#429716 3 2008-04-19T01:51:37Z Strife
What a coincidence - we just had two reps from the RIAA at my university for a question and answer session.

On the subject of piracy in general, not specifically PC games, I think trying to curb piracy is a respectable and necessary task, however messy. Despite the arguments about the questionable legality of making your own copy of a piece of software or media, the fact remains that piracy has a very significant detrimental effect on the market for these things. People are losing their jobs smply because there is that much less money coming in.

But I'm a little different in terms of music - I'll happily pay an artist for an original reproduction of their work, but I feel less satisfied about doing the same for a copy. I pay for food because each piece of food is an original craft. Otherwise, if I go out and purchase materials to construct for myself a copy of something I see, then I've already paid in materials and time for that product, and the fact that I can make it myself means I don't need you to sell it to me. But I can't copy experience - if you want to put on a concert, I'll pay for that, but I'm not paying you $10 to click "burn another copy" and pop another CD in the drive, because I can buy the raw materials and the tools and do it myself. Thanks for the creativity and all, but I can take it from here. If you want me to pay you at some point, though, then you have to be able to do a better job than I can, and enough to convince me that I need your version.

I can't think of a suitable segue...
Here are some of the questions asked to those RIAA reps:

A company in your employ, MediaSentry, was issued a cease and desist order on January 2nd by the MA state police, yet there is evidence that they have continued surveillance, directly in contradiction to the terms on the order. How can the RIAA lecture students on legal and moral issues when they violate personal rights?

It has come up that your partners do not wish to disclose the methods they use to find file sharers online. Do you believe it is right and fair for someone to be forced to accept that your methods are correct on faith, especially when they have been proven faulty before?

How can you continue to pursue file sharers when there has already been one person fully exonerated from your charges (Atlantic v. Andersen)?

After having your expert witness embarassed on the stand in UMG v. Lindor, and having other so-called "expert" witnesses disbarred in other courts (specifically in the Netherlands in 2005), how can you continue with these cases?
(Examples:
* expert witness kept no detailed record of his search
* expert witness made serious errors concerning how certain parts of a network function
* expert witness failed to document his forensics, which he stated is not necessary and any other professional would consider okay
* expert witness claimed to be an expert on MediaSentry but was unable to discuss its methods or potential flaws
* expert witness claimed that the screenshot evidence proved his case, admitted they could be easily manipulated, and proceeded with testimony anyway
* expert witness admits that forensic inspection of the defendant's hard drive revealed nothing suggestive of illicit filesharing)

On April 2nd, the University of Maine filed a Rule 11 Motion in the case Arista v. Does. part of their motion states that the RIAA's pursuit of student filesharers violates FERPA. How do you justify circumventing FERPA (privacy) regulations? Do you believe it would be fair for Student Health Services to turn over my records, in violation of HIPPA?

Another portion of the Rule 11 Motion in Arista v. Does alleges that the RIAA v. Does is designed to intimidate people into settling. why are you still railroading people into lucrative pre-litigation settlements when you state your initial intention is to sue and not settle?


There were a handful of other questions asked, and the RIAA reps didn't walk away very happy.

But still, kids... stealing is wrong.

#429753 10 2008-04-19T05:54:07Z Strife
On a purely practical level, induction has been perfectly functional in the entire realm of human experience. Refusing to believe that everything you know about reality might spontaneously change because you aren't 100% certain that it won't is not a healthy mindset, even if it is technically correct. (Ask me why I don't call myself an agnostic.) Billions of people have lived their lives just fine assuming that gravitation will not change, so I'm going to do that too, even though I technically have no reason to believe that my circumstances will be similar once you point out that induction is circular reasoning.
I fully understand that this isn't helpful for your paper, but I also think it's a waste of time to find any guarantees in this universe.

#429884 12 2008-04-19T15:20:36Z Strife
Quote: Tapp, April 19, 2008 07:48 am
Induction can be less useful at smaller scales, as quantum physics shows.
Quantum logic is thought to be the true one now, it is essentially induction except with some leeway (at plank scale) depending on odds. So, for example, you could drop a ball and almost all of the time it will fall, so we can deduce the odds of it happening the same way again. What's different is having no complete certainty, giving leeway for such phenomena. It's worked so far.

...

You don't just run into induction in quantum mechanics. You run into it as early as you start to recognize patterns in reality, which happens before you're even self-aware.
You run into the problem of induction when you expect 1 + 1 to remain equal to 2 simply because that's what was true earlier.

1 + 1 = 2 is maybe not the best example. I mean, everyone acknowledges that you learn math through empirical/inductive means, but I can expect one and one to equal two forever because of the meaning of the word "one." I think that that kind of mathematical statement is deductive, not inductive.

#20086 1 2008-04-26T21:40:11Z Strife
As you may have guessed from the title of this thread, I work in an IT department, specifically for a university. It's a paid job, but the service we provide is free for students and faculty. We take phone calls as well as walk-in cases, and they're handled at our desks.
One of my coworkers received a particularly difficult call - the caller was very impatient, insulting, threatening, and even screamed a bit, too. This coworker remained polite and cheery as ever on the call, and then commented out loud about the call she had received. I, being the sort of person to bring up my opinion of the subject in casual conversation, brought up my opinion of the subject in casual conversation. A customer overheard me and politely objected, and then we discussed it a bit.
...in case you're wondering what inspired this thread.

I define [being paid to take your crap] as receiving monetary compensation from you for performing a service for you. If you're not paying me, and no amount of your money is in my paycheck, then I technically don't owe you a thing.
I also define [being paid [i]enough[/i] to take your crap] as receiving such an amount of monetary compensation from you, directly or indirectly (e.g. through my employer), that I, personally, deem is worth the trouble you give me.

Let's say I start up a service where I hire people to help you when you request it. I pay them out of my own pocket, and I don't charge for the help in the slightest. In this situation, there is absolutely no difference in the benefit I receive whether I help you or not, because you're not going to recompensate me whether I assist you or not. In other words, doing business with you, no matter who you are, is exactly as beneficial as not doing business at all. This means that I'm not obligated to help you do anything, and that the service I'm paying people to provide for you is me being benevolent, me acting out of the goodness of my heart. If you come and request the service, I said I would perform it, so I probably will. I make no promises, and you should understand that a free service means that I'm completely free from liability, but I'll probably do it because I said I would. But if you're being exceedingly difficult, then I have no incentive whatsoever to put up with you, and my associates and I are not going to be affected in the slightest if I refuse to provide you the service I said I'd provide. In fact, in being difficult, you may be taking up valuable time I could be spending on customers who will be grateful, so it may even be a net benefit to ignore you.

Let's reduce this even further:
Let's say I offer a service wherein I, if asked, will pick up something you have dropped, and maybe even help you carry it. I don't charge for this service, and it in fact probably inconveniences me, so I'm performing it out of the goodness of my heart.
If you drop your book and ask me to pick it up for you, I will probably do it if I'm legitimately making the offer. But if you drop your book and say, "hey asshole, pick up my book," then you can go to hell as far as I'm concerned. If you want to pay me $20 and say "hey asshole, pick up my book," then that's fine - you're effectively making the offer, "can I pay you $20 to remain unaffected by my calling you an asshole," which is an entirely different offer (one I'd probably take), in addition to the service I provide for free. But otherwise, there is no incentive whatsoever for me to help you if you're consciously being difficult.

I think it's fairly safe to say that if you take advantage of a free service - even if the workers are being paid - and you're consciously being difficult to deal with, then you should expect to be treated in kind. In some cases, such as if your opinion is influential and those providing the service would somehow lose money if you had a bad experience, or even if the workers are being paid enough, then they will still be polite to you regardless, but this is not something you should expect. Otherwise, what am I, as the proprietor of the service, supposed to be afraid of? That you'll call my supervisor and have him explain to you how your business means nothing to us? That you'll go off and tell your friends to not use my free service so that I can't get their $0 as well? Even if you cut the customer base down to 10%, our revenue is still $0. Give me a break.

In my case, I'm being paid by the IT Department, which actually makes most of its money from sales, and only a very small fraction of your tuition as a student comes to the IT Department at all. As in the previous example ($20, "asshole," etc.), I may decide that the amount you're offering to pay me to put up with you just isn't enough. So if you pay your tuition, a fraction of a cent of which probably comes to me in my entire duration of employment, I may very well decide that that isn't enough for me to put up with you.

Obviously, an employer can be dissatisfied with employees who give the minimum expected (no service whatsoever), but such employees would be fired because they're wasting money, not because they're making the employer lose any money (because they aren't). The employer-employee relationship is that the employee is an extension of the employer, so if the employer feels that an employee isn't carrying out the employer's intentions, the employee is going to be fired because he's vestigial, but not because he's bringing harm to the organization as a whole (because he isn't).

Clearly, being a liability as an employee is bad. Don't misunderstand and think I'm saying that employees should feel free to be a liability for the employer or the customer, because the service is no longer free in such a case ("we fix your computer, and all you have to do is take a lot of crap from us"). If you're being paid to sit on your hands, then you are a liability for your employer (he is giving you money for absolutely nothing in return) and you should expect to be fired.

As an IT guy who offers tech support that you hardly pay a penny for, I try to help everyone, but if you're being exceedingly difficult (and I stress "exceedingly," because there are naturally more patient guys than me who would like my job), then I won't give you the cheery/preppy/polite treatment I give to the grateful customers.

#434823 8 2008-05-09T19:18:16Z Strife
Quote: ZZ9, 2008-05-09T18:13:51Z
No, because your nervous system doesn't develop until about 4 months into the pregnancy. For the first part, it depends whether you mean exist as an thing, or exist as an individual.
On those same lines, I define myself as my consciousness. I'm not conscious until I have a sufficiently complex and functional nervous system that would allow me to be conscious.
As such, I say my body existed well before I did. And depending upon what happens to me and my body, either my consciousness - and therefore I - will die before my body stops functioning (Alzheimer's, miscellaneous dementia, etc.), or I'll die at about the same time my body dies. Because my nervous system will no longer be in a condition to support consciousness (it'll be inert matter when my body is dead), I don't expect to exist after my body's death at the very latest.

But if you believe in a soul, God, ghosts, or any number of other religious ideas, then you've probably invented your own arbitrary, nifty-sounding, completely untestable theory about when you existed.

"I believe that a race of magical space-elves are the creators and maintainers of the human species. They piece every one of us together by choosing hundreds of elements of our personality from the Personality Pool and balancing our negative and positive character traits so that our Complete User Neutrality Temperance index is zero. They inject our unique personality into a base consisting of a magical space-elf soul held in stasis with super condensed ectoplasm, a state called Personal Ectoplasmic Nascence-Inhibiting Stasis. Then they pack the soul into a transdimensional transport in a device that will fully evaporate the ectoplasm (the Time Indexed Hyper Sleep Longetivity Lengthening Umbilical Buffer) by the time the soul tesseracts into a pregnant woman's womb."

#435274 22 2008-05-11T18:23:27Z Strife
maestro: That was me, not Tsukatu. This account, not my other.

Again, if you believe in something like a soul or spirit, or anything else that you think contributes to or is your personality, character, consciousness, etc., then you can feel free to believe that you existed before your body did.
...because at that point logic can be happily tossed out the window.

But if you believe as I do that there is nothing else that contains what is called "you" other than the arrangement and specific function of neural matter, then I think it's unreasonable to believe that "you" could exist before your body. Or after it, for that matter.
Sure, your genetic code exists long before you do (as well as components thereof, before your conception), but those plans haven't yet been realized.
If you understand object-oriented programming, saying that you exist simply because there are plans for the neural network that would then produce "you" is like saying that an instance of a class exists simply because there is a class definition, and that's obviously not the case.

#438995 64 2008-05-25T08:52:18Z Strife
bobaganuesh_2.
Please stop talking immediately.
Please.

There are so, so many things wrong with what you're saying. I really, truly hope that you don't actually believe any of what you said, or believe that it works that way... that anything works that way. I can't imagine the depth of ignorance it would take to believe that you could take a scientific formula and just swap in completely irrelevant abstract nouns and still have it work.

Yes, in a closed, isolated system, momentum is conserved.
"lb" is meaningless in science, for starters. You'd have to quantify it as something; you can't just say "life" as a variable without it having at least some magnitude. You can't swap out whatever you want into an equation; you can only swap in something equivalent via substitution. In this case, mass * velocity is not equivalent to "life." I mean, I don't know what the hell kind of units you're giving to "life," but I'm willing to bet it isn't kilogram-meters per second. The second part of your equation ("life after") describes something supernatural, in other words, something entirely untestable, unexaminable, and unobserved by any human science ever. It does not belong in any scientific equation, especially, as just discussed, as a substitute for a completely irrelevant term.

And you do realize that calling 'mv' by one variable, 'p', doesn't actually change anything, right? It's equal because it's exactly the same thing; you could write 'mv' anywhere you could write 'p', and 'p' anywhere 'mv'. They're interchangable. You don't do anything magical or special by saying 'p=mv'. Nothing at all.

As for the "curve" of the universe, you're really gonna have to give us some units on them axes before we can explain how thoroughly wrong you are on that front.

I'm trying to think of a term that's more pseudo than pseudoscience. Superpseudo-science maybe?
#439133 86 2008-05-25T21:53:13Z Strife
On a purely technical note, brocerius, you may not want to say "there is nothing beyond infinity," because that implies that infinity is actually some boundary, some limit. Infinity is defined as a mathematical limit, a different concept entirely from the conventional definition of 'limit'.

#440706 2 2008-05-31T08:06:44Z Strife
It sounds like settings aren't being saved; make sure you have write permissions to all of the game files.

Alternatively...
Amusingly enough, the most success in playing N in linux for a number of users here has been running the Windows version with WINE.
apt-cache search wine should bring up brief package information, but then again I'm using Ubuntu 8.04 (Hardy Heron), so I'm searching in the hardy repositories. I don't remember if wine joined the default Ubuntu repositories before then. If you can't find it, make sure you uncomment the "universe" and "multiverse" entries in /etc/apt/sources.list, do sudo aptitude update, and then search again. If that doesn't list it, go to http://www.winehq.org

sudo aptitude install wine
wine [wherever]/n_v14.exe
...although you may want to give the game highest priority:
sudo nice -n 20 wine [wherever]/n_v14.exe

You may be given grief about a particular C++ library (I was back when I tried it for the first time in Ubuntu 5.10). If that's the case, just Google the library name, download it, put it into /usr/lib or wherever the executable is looking for it, and you should be all set.

#441385 2 2008-06-02T22:02:41Z Strife
By and large the most useful math skills in programming are discrete things (no pesky decimals or fractions... most of the time). Off the top of my head, I'd recommend Set Theory most highly, as well as Linear Algebra (matrix operations). A smattering of Calculus, particularly multivariate, would help greatly if you plan on doing anything 3D, although a few off-hand topics covered in calculus like series and sequence calculations may end up being useful every now and again.
Other than that, you don't *really* need much complicated unless you're trying to make the game uber-realistic. Super Mario has its own very simplistic laws of physics, whereas Mare and Raigan put in some hardcore mechanical physics expertise to work in making N.

#20467 1 2008-06-07T21:45:01Z Strife
I've been in Cambridge, MA for the last few days for my brother's graduation from MIT (my brother is Gordon Freeman). The commencement ceremony was about 4 hours, unless you wanted any seating whatsoever, in which case it was closer to 4 hours of ceremony + 2 hours of waiting.
Out of all members of my family, I'm chosen to arrive early and reserve seats. So I arrive at 8:30 AM or so, about an hour after they started letting people in (commencement began at 9:45 AM), and I managed to get some decent seats. The weather forecast for the day was "slightly cloudy," so I only brought a light jacket in the case of light rain. About half an hour in, it started to rain very heavily. My jacket, despite the word "weatherproof" written on the inside, turned out not to be so weatherproof. Wondering who the hell thought that a commencement ceremony held outside in Boston was at any point a good idea, I ran to get one of the ponchos being handed out by the staff. By the time I get back to my seats, they're of course drenched with water, and the rag I used to wipe them off from the last night's rain was thoroughly soaked and useless. My poncho was just short enough that my butt was uncovered by it when I sat down. Figuring I'm already soaked anyway, I did my best to clear as much water as I could from the seat before sitting down and soaking my ass. The rain stops falling at around 9:20, and my family shows up at 9:30. They're mostly dry, whereas I'm thoroughly soaked (especially from the jackasses in front of me who kept tilting their umbrellas back, dumping a bunch of water onto my lap). No one behind the third row can see anything anyway because 70% of the crowd decided that bringing umbrellas wasn't discourteous.
The graduates begin marching down the center aisle at around 10:15, led by some official in fancy robes who was carrying a huge golden mace (the Mace of Knowledge? not a very reasonable item to represent higher learning, is it?). And this line of graduates was enormous. They just did not stop coming. Their number was somewhere in the hundreds. Once everyone was in their seats, we got a few speeches from various people, all pretty much saying, "be good people." The only insight I gained from those speeches was that the guest speaker, Mohammad Yunus, was eleven times more awesome than I previously thought. These speeches lasted something around 45 minutes. Then it was time to name all of the graduates. Even though they had two people going at once, one naming undergrads and the other naming graduate students, it still took until about 2:00 PM for them to finish. And my family had to stay around to the end because my brother's major and name put him second-to-last in the order. Then we went around to receptions, and that was just fine.

tl;dr...
Graduation ceremonies are intensely boring. Every family in the audience only cared about one or two graduates, and each graduate only had five people at most in the audience who knew who he was or cared that he was graduating. And yet the entire audience was subjected to the naming of every single one of the hundreds upon hundreds of graduates. This all just seemed unnecessary to me.
So I'm thinking, when I'm in my final year, that I'll make some unchangeable plans for the day of graduation and ask that my diploma simply be mailed to me. Instead of spending my happy day waiting for hours, hungry, in a rain-soaked, fold-out chair, wearing a dress whose color is leaking into my clothing, and messing up my shoes in the mud, I'll invite my friends and family over and have a private graduation party. That way, all the people who don't care won't have to endure me and my family, and I and my family won't have to endure all of the people who don't care. I can't think of a single reason to go with the commencement ceremony over a private party. Not a one. And this extends to all ceremonies where the audience and the participants are only invested in 0.001% of the entire event.

Comments?

#444043 70 2008-06-12T21:38:09Z Strife
Quote: Turtle, 2008-06-12T16:21:38Z
Don't you think it would be a little boring for both God and for the people if the world was completely perfect?
What do you think Heaven is?

"1. First of all, I recall to your attention the extraordinary fact with which I began. To wit, that the human being, like the immortals, naturally places sexual intercourse far and away above all other joys -- yet he has left it out of his heaven! The very thought of it excites him; opportunity sets him wild; in this state he will risk life, reputation, everything -- even his queer heaven itself -- to make good that opportunity and ride it to the overwhelming climax. From youth to middle age all men and all women prize copulation above all other pleasures combined, yet it is actually as I have said: it is not in their heaven; prayer takes its place.
...
2. In man's heaven everybody sings! The man who did not sing on earth sings there; the man who could not sing on earth is able to do it there. The universal singing is not casual, not occasional, not relieved by intervals of quiet; it goes on, all day long, and every day, during a stretch of twelve hours. And everybody stays; whereas in the earth the place would be empty in two hours. The singing is of hymns alone. Nay, it is of one hymn alone. The words are always the same, in number they are only about a dozen, there is no rhyme, there is no poetry: "Hosannah, hosannah, hosannah, Lord God of Sabaoth, 'rah! 'rah! 'rah! siss! -- boom! ... a-a-ah!"

3. Meantime, every person is playing on a harp -- those millions and millions! -- whereas not more than twenty in the thousand of them could play an instrument in the earth, or ever wanted to.
Consider the deafening hurricane of sound -- millions and millions of voices screaming at once and millions and millions of harps gritting their teeth at the same time! I ask you: is it hideous, is it odious, is it horrible?

Consider further: it is a praise service; a service of compliment, of flattery, of adulation! Do you ask who it is that is willing to endure this strange compliment, this insane compliment; and who not only endures it, but likes it, enjoys it, requires if, commands it? Hold your breath!

It is God! This race's god, I mean. He sits on his throne, attended by his four and twenty elders and some other dignitaries pertaining to his court, and looks out over his miles and miles of tempestuous worshipers, and smiles, and purrs, and nods his satisfaction northward, eastward, southward; as quaint and nave a spectacle as has yet been imagined in this universe, I take it.
...
All sane white people hate noise; yet they have tranquilly accepted this kind of heaven -- without thinking, without reflection, without examination -- and they actually want to go to it! Profoundly devout old gray-headed men put in a large part of their time dreaming of the happy day when they will lay down the cares of this life and enter into the joys of that place. Yet you can see how unreal it is to them, and how little it takes a grip upon them as being fact, for they make no practical preparation for the great change: you never see one of them with a harp, you never hear one of them sing.

As you have seen, that singular show is a service of praise: praise by hymn, praise by prostration. It takes the place of "church." Now then, in the earth these people cannot stand much church -- an hour and a quarter is the limit, and they draw the line at once a week. That is to say, Sunday. One day in seven; and even then they do not look forward to it with longing. And so -- consider what their heaven provides for them: "church" that lasts forever, and a Sabbath that has no end! They quickly weary of this brief hebdomadal Sabbath here, yet they long for that eternal one; they dream of it, they talk about it, they think they think they are going to enjoy it -- with all their simple hearts they think they think they are going to be happy in it!
...
5. Every man in the earth possesses some share of intellect, large or small; and be it large or be it small he takes pride in it. Also his heart swells at mention of the names of the majestic intellectual chiefs of his race, and he loves the tale of their splendid achievements. For he is of their blood, and in honoring themselves they have honored him. Lo, what the mind of man can do! he cries, and calls the roll of the illustrious of all ages; and points to the imperishable literatures they have given to the world, and the mechanical wonders they have invented, and the glories wherewith they have clothed science and the arts; and to them he uncovers as to kings, and gives to them the profoundest homage, and the sincerest, his exultant heart can furnish -- thus exalting intellect above all things else in the world, and enthroning it there under the arching skies in a supremacy unapproachable. And then he contrived a heaven that hasn't a rag of intellectuality in it anywhere!

Is it odd, is it curious, is it puzzling? It is exactly as I have said, incredible as it may sound. This sincere adorer of intellect and prodigal rewarder of its mighty services here in the earth has invented a religion and a heaven which pay no compliments to intellect, offer it no distinctions, fling it no largess: in fact, never even mention it.

By this time you will have noticed that the human being's heaven has been thought out and constructed upon an absolute definite plan; and that this plan is, that it shall contain, in labored detail, each and every imaginable thing that is repulsive to a man, and not a single thing he likes!

Very well, the further we proceed the more will this curious fact be apparent.

Make a note of it: in man's heaven there are no exercises for the intellect, nothing for it to live upon. It would rot there in a year -- rot and stink. Rot and stink -- and at that stage become holy. A blessed thing: for only the holy can stand the joys of that bedlam."
--Satan, Letters from Earth, by Mark Twain

#444592 6 2008-06-14T22:36:45Z Strife
As you probably already know, I'm an antitheist. My exact explanation of what that means for me has had some variation with time, but here it is at its most recent:
As an antitheist, I believe that those religions of the world I know of cannot be correct. I don't simply lack belief like an atheist; I posit that they are false, impossible. Furthermore, in addition to this traditional antitheistic view, my thoughts these days are that no belief involving a supernatural and/or transcendent being or force is correct; such a thing cannot exist. I also believe that religious convictions are harmful to society and to the individual, and I regard religion as a mental disease that can be cured with education.
I'm currently reading God: The Failed Hypothesis, which claims to disprove God through science, which confused me at first because I didn't think that science and God overlapped in the slightest... but it's making sense so far. I don't know if it will adjust my view of things.

Quote: SkyPanda, 2008-06-14T16:48:24Z
Just about all creation theories and the like, whether religious or scientific, are mind blowingly stupid
Y'know, I'm getting really sick of people putting Creation and the Big Bang on the same level in terms of plausibility, because it's a clear sign that whoever is making such a hugely ignorant comparison hasn't made a minimal effort to look into the Big Bang. Let me guess - you think, "first there was nothing, and then that nothing exploded... sounds like rubbish," am I right?
DID YOU KNOW!?
Science doesn't just make shit up and then press it upon the rest of the world?
It's true! The reason the Big Bang is so popular is mostly because of the COBE mission, which was one of the most fantastically successful scientific breakthroughs in the history of science and mankind - the scientists who developed the theory that the universe had an explosive beginning (note the word "developed," as in, "they thought other things first, and the overwhelming number of reasons to believe it had an explosive beginning led them to the hypothesis that is now the Big Bang") said that if they were correct the universe's background radiation would look something like a graph they made. Then they launched COBE and, lo and behold, it was exactly as they predicted. It really couldn't have been more perfect a fit.
And if you're telling me that this is comparable to "magic man done it," with proof ranging from "so a watch has a watch-maker, right?" to "this here magic book says it," then I have nothing hatred for the way you and people like you think.

The point of that example is simply to say that if you have no reason to believe one potentially made-up thing over another but you do believe the universe was created, then you shouldn't be so certain about the creator's qualities. That is, Christians believe in a creator who has taken a very specific set of actions as the Bible tells... but I say it's just as likely that magical space elves made it, and the Bible is just something someone made up, so the actual creators of the universe are unknown to us.

If you're familiar with Russell's Teapot, it's enough to say "there's a teapot floating in space," but it's quite another to say, "there's a teapot floating in space. It's green with eleven pink polka dots all around the edges. Its spout is 4 inches long, and it has a hemi-elliptical handle with an eccentricity of 0.87. In 2000 BCE, it spun counter-clockwise 48 degrees and flew away from the sun eleven meters, and then raised its top and circled around the sun one radian in its orbit. Then it stopped, spun in place four hundred times, closed its top and opened it again, and turned into a dragon. Then it exploded into millions of tiny teapots, which did a lap around Alpha Centauri and then reassembled into the original teapot, only blue this time. Then it enlightened some desert nomads and declared its hatred for homosexuality."
In other words, it takes quite an unreasonable leap of faith to believe in creation in the first place (the ideal position at this point is, "I don't know"), but when you append the statement of your belief with a Bible-length of actions inspired by this creator, you've gone completely off the deep end.

Do you know the origin of Intelligent Design, incidentally? An investigation into the origin of this phrase was started during the Dover, Pennsylvania Intelligent Design trials, and they looked at a book called Pandas And People. After getting their hands on old copies of the book, they found a version that was exactly like one just after, except that every instance of the phrase "intelligent design" was changed from "creation." Hilariously enough, there was even an evolutionary link found between the two phrases: someone had done a very hasty "find and replace" and managed to change the word "creationists" into "cdesign proponentsists" (go ahead and Google that phrase) - Creationism has evolved into Intelligent Design!

On to this nasty omnipotence / free will business:
Quote: Keron Cyst, 2008-06-17T02:51:45Z
[Determinists say that] you were ultimately, given the events of your past (which shaped your personality, beliefs, and ideals and gave you the physical, emotional, and mental stability you currently bear in you, your physical body), you "had no choice" but to ultimately come upon this thread...
Just to get this out of the way, yes, this is what I believe.

Quote: Keron Cyst, 2008-06-17T02:51:45Z
Aren't you willingly doing this? You are reading this because you want to, regardless of whether you are masochistically procrastinating or are just bored, or consider this a relevant, substantial issue to devote your time to; you're doing this of your own free will.
As far as my consciousness can immediately realize, yes, it appears that I am doing this willingly. But that's not how brains work; humans have the illusion of choice.
I may have said this before:
How Brains Work:
You are your sub-consious self. Every thought you think, every movement you make, every emotion you feel is your sub-conscious supercomputer tallying activation energies within your neuron pathways, and your body moves and reacts to its environment like a super-sophisticated clock. Your consciousness, on the other hand, is its own creature that sits back and watches what your sub-conscious is doing, and it only retroactively justifies its actions to itself, and then has this delusion that it's actually in control of anything. If some external stimulus made your nose itch, your nose sends the "I'm itchy" signal to your brain, which responds with a my.Hand.dominantHand().scratch(my.Nose); Your consciousness, on the other hand, processes "my hand is moving; why is my hand moving? It went to my nose and scratched it. Oh, I must've been itching there," and ends up with "my nose was itching, so I decided to scratch it," when in fact no part of your reaction went through your prefrontal lobe in the slightest. From the point your nose's nerves sent the signal to your somatosensory cortex that it was itchy to your motor cortex's signal to your hand (with help from the cerebellum for coordination) to scratch your nose, none of it relies in any way on what the prefrontal cortex thinks of all this. It's relayed to it because your consciousness is nagging it to tell it what's going on, but the prefrontal only digests this information internally, and doesn't do much in the way of influencing the scratching. If your clockwork subconscious decides to be obstinate and not scratch your nose, your consciousness, while confused for a nanosecond, will surmise that it commanded the subconscious not to do anything. Either way, your consciousness is just watching a movie, but thinks that it's playing a game.
So no, I have the illusion of choice, but I don't actually have any say in this.

Quote: Keron Cyst, 2008-06-17T02:51:45Z

#448267 6 2008-06-24T18:50:24Z Strife
I was somewhat in your shoes a few years back (as Dave can testify, in fact), but I moved past it.

I will agree with you on a few key points. Yes, voluntarily poisoning yourself is retarded. Yes, alcohol is damaging to your body. Yes, people find the most clever of ways to do stupid things. Yes, drunk driving is a serious problem.
Despite these things, however, I continue to drink. While I realize that poisoning myself is bad, I do plenty of other things that harm me - I eat fast food, I don't exercise regularly, I spend a lot of time out of the sunlight, I don't get enough sleep, I have an unbalanced diet, etc., and all of these things actually contribute to my enjoyment. Fast food is tasty, exercise is labor, the sun annoys me, and the things I do instead of sleep are also enjoyable . Alcohol is the same for me in that I do think it benefits me enough that I'll take the detriment as well. Like Demonz, I don't drink much when I do, and I don't do it often at all, but it actually does have a meaningful impact on my social skills. I remain easily sober enough to act reasonably and to react to emergencies, but I lose just enough of my social inhibitions that I'm friendlier, more patient, and all around more charismatic. I think that's worth it.
And I've been on the other extreme, and it isn't fun (for me, anyway) being completely trashed. I'm less in control of myself, I look like an idiot, and people lose respect for me. That's not something I'm interested in, so I don't drink so much. But I know plenty of people who drink to this point willingly, even intentionally, so I think I share your dislike of people who are all about getting wasted at parties. I'd advise you to avoid these people if I thought you weren't already.

As for the health risks, I don't drink enough to destroy any part of my brain that I'll miss. Maybe in 20 years I'll take an extra 0.3 seconds to multiply two two-digit numbers in my head or something, but I really don't care. And if you lose 90% of your liver before you're 30, you'll grow it all back. The psychological benefits are worth all of that to me.

But I do also agree with you that it's all very silly that alcohol became so popular in the first place. I think there are plenty of safer alternatives that people could use to reduce their social inhibitions that isn't quite so dangerous as alcohol, but our ridiculous legal system disagrees for all the wrong reasons. If said substances were legal, I don't think I'd even touch alcohol. I think that beer companies in particular have done an epic marketing job by injecting alcohol into our culture, sort of like what Coca-Cola did with Santa Claus. I think people would see alcohol in the same light as marijuana if it hadn't been for years of marketing genius.

Driving under the influence should be legally tried as attempted murder.

#449218 81 2008-06-28T00:13:06Z Strife
Quote: incluye, June 27, 2008 02:47 pm
How would any past occurences of nature predict our future actions? (This is what I've interpreted from Daddaluma.) That makes absolutely no sense to me.
"Unbreakable laws of physics" is the answer.
Let's say you have a pool table with a bunch of balls on it, and you hit the cue really hard into another ball. Based on the velocity and the angle at which it hit the other ball, the balls would roll around and hit each other in a certain way, and every single one of these is predictable with simple math and a little understanding of mechanical physics. If you were to then set up the table exactly as before and hit the cue ball exactly the same way, then you'd notice that exactly the same thing happens: the end state is the same. No matter how many times you do this, the end state will always be the same as long as you put the table back into the starting state and hit the cue exactly the same way.
Now think of your brain as a pool table hundreds of thousands of miles to a side, with hundreds of billions of balls. Some stimulus comes along, grabs a pool stick, and hits the cue a certain way, and this causes a flurry of motion among the balls. But the thing is, the paths of every single ball is still quite predictable, and if you were to set each and every one of the balls exactly the same way and ask the stimulus to hit the cue the same way he did the first time, you would always end up with the same end state.
So if you're standing in a room, picking your nose and staring at a wall, and then someone throws a baseball at your crotch, you'd react to the ball by covering your groin, widening your eyes, releasing adrenaline into your bloodstream, etc. And then if you were to turn back time so that you were back in the same position again, picking your nose exactly the same way, staring at exactly the same spot on the wall as before, and with no memory of the ball, and then the ball is thrown at your crotch again in exactly the same way and at exactly the same time as before, then your reaction will be completely, 100% identical to your previous reaction: you'll take just as long to put your hands over your groin in exactly the same manner, your eyes will widen just as wide as before in exactly the same amount of time, and your adrenal glands will swell just as much and just as quickly as they did last time. This will happen no matter how many times you reverse time and play it forward.
THIS MEANS that your outcome is defined entirely by your constituent bits (namely the subatomic particles that make up every atom of every cell of every tissue that you'd consider part of your body) and how they react to the quite rigid laws of physics. If put in such a situation, your quarks will react exactly the same way every single time. You have as much a say in what you do as the 7 ball does when it's hit by the pool cue, which is to say you have no choice in what happens to you whatsoever.
And by extension, every stimulus was caused by something else and moved in a predictable way to stimulate you, and if you trace back all of those causes, you'll realize that things could only have ended up a single way since the beginning of time, just as everything that happens on the pool table is determined the moment the cue ball is struck (the balls can only move a certain way, and that's exactly what they'll do). Everything about you, every thing you think, every emotion you feel, every nervous tick and virus you get, every way you influence your surroundings and people around you, has been determined at the beginning of time.

That's determinism in a nutshell, and science can back it up fully so far (and that doesn't look like it's going to change).

#449826 3 2008-06-30T22:55:06Z Strife
Quote: incluye, 2008-06-30T19:07:17Z
Mac's design, hardware, and programming are superior in my view.
Only in that they function better than something notoriously dysfunctional, not to even mention that Mac OS X is completely incapable of doing much of what Vista can do in the first place.

I'll agree that UNIX is more stable than Windows, and that it comes with a number of tools that I'm shocked Windows doesn't have (primarily basic networking tools like ssh and sftp, or a C compiler that comes with the OS), but Apple more than makes up for the benefits it has over Windows by being overbearingly fascist.
A huge portion of the features Mac OS X holds over Windows' head have already been a part of Linux for years - remember the part where Mac OS X is based on UNIX? Many more features are simply eye candy that Linux window managers and Windows third-party applications can replicate, and the only "convenience" is that you're forced to waste disk space and CPU power on them in Mac OS X if you don't want them. One of the most effort-intensive projects for Mac OS X is emulation of Windows programs and applications... if Mac OS X is so robust and superior, why is this the case? Why can't Mac applications sneer at the inferior Windows software packages and force Windows to make a compatibility layer to use Mac applications just so it can survive in the market? Because so far, the idea that Mac OS X is that superior to Windows doesn't hold up to practice; practice shows that just the opposite is the case, that Mac OS X has survived in the market because it finds decently stable ways to run Windows software.
On that note, I don't remember the last time I saw a commercial for Windows that venomously attacked Apple out of bitter jealousy and spite.
But those aren't even my biggest gripes with Apple Inc. No, what really grinds my gears is how restrictive Apple is when it comes to extensibility and basic customization. Want to change the look of Windows or Linux? Go right ahead! Linux even gives you the source code so you can hack away at the foundation as much as you please. Want to change the look of Mac OS X? Too goddamned bad. Apple is so restrictive about this that even the software you write for the platform has to have the Mac OS X look. This is effectively declaring, "this is what 'creative' looks like; if your software doesn't look like this, then it isn't creative." That's about as ridiculous as a museum giving a restrictive form factor for art. "This is what a painting is. The canvas must be at least two feet on a side, and you must use light shades of grey for the majority of the painting. Your painting must include a red circle, a green circle, and a yellow circle in the top right corner, otherwise it is not a painting and is unacceptable." Apple is the only OS to release software development kits that were specifically made to not be platform-independent. Given that software compatibility is based primarily on the CPU, and given that the overwhelming majority of computers on the market right now use a processor that can support both Mac OS X and your favorite flavor of Windows, Apple Inc really had to go out of their way to pull such a dick move.
I'll even grant that Mac OS X is just as functional, if not more so due to the stability provided by the UNIX base, as Windows at performing the majority of tasks a user would want, but only if you stay within Mac OS X. If you want to edit movies, edit music, manage your contacts, whatever, Mac OS X has some pretty powerful software to do just that, but so does Windows (incidentally, Photoshop CS3 runs smoother in Windows than in OS X), and the difference between those tasks in Windows and OS X is that Windows doesn't force you to save content in proprietary Windows formats that are only workable ever again exclusively within Windows. As soon as any company steps forward as Apple has and declares, "you can only do things our way, and we're going to force you to even if you don't want to," they have officially described themselves as evil. Apple is very hostile to creative third-party software and to changing the way it looks or operates, absolutely refuses to allow any other hardware manufacturer to produce OEM machines with their OS, and doesn't even trust their own consumers to change the batteries on their iPhones. That's another critical issue: Apple has historically demonstrated it has no confidence whatsoever in the competence of its user base.
Probably the dumbest argument I've heard to date is that Mac OS X is so much more aesthetically pleasing than Windows. The reason this is so dumb is because Windows can easily change the way it looks to just about anything, and Mac OS X can't. This means that the look and feel of Mac OS X is a subset of what Windows is capable of, and there are probably hundreds of variations of themes for Windows out on the web that make your Windows OS look exactly like Mac OS X, but not vice-versa. I happen to think that Vista looks really pretty - why shouldn't I be able to make Mac OS X look like Vista? It's my computer, isn't it?
Every time I've used Mac OS X, I've still felt that I wasn't in control of my own computer. Hunter Cressel said it best: "I don't feel like I'm operating the Mac so much as I'm just there sharing the Mac experience and if I can do something useful while the Mac is willing, so much the better." [source]
Other favorite quotes include, "It feels like there's a wall of Jello between me and the work I'm trying to do" and "I still haven't given up completely on OSX but it's a very far cry from the 'just works' OS that I was sold on. Its sluggish UI, insistence on following it's own narrow-sighted metaphors, and the insular nature of it's applications has made me certain to avoid a Mac the next time I buy a system." [source]
And as if that wasn't enough, Apple completely abuses the fan base it doesn't deserve by overcharging them at every opportunity. Apple refuses to allow other hardware manufacturers to sell OEM Mac OS X machines so that Apple can get the profits of an insane markup on hardware, which is sometimes upwards of 1000%. Every now and again, Macfags gather together in a dark room where they have the cult leader unveil the next product they have to support unconditionally (the iPhone didn't have extensible storage, customizable ringtones with your own mp3's, video recording, support for MMS messages, voice dialing, voice recording, or instant messaging, all of which modern phones going for 20% of the cost included by default). The end result is that you have a single person dictating the progress of a huge field of human research, communication, creativity, and entertainment by providing shoddy products under his own unreasonably stubborn standards for what is acceptable, all the while convincing them to continue emptying their wallets for him when they can get better (or at least equal), unrestricted benefits for a much lower price. That's a cult. That's why I call Apple Inc a cult.
I know you're happy using a Mac. If that works for you, then fine. Just keep in mind that plenty of Scientologists are equally happy because they also think their investment is worth it.

If you want to compare the Apple philosophy, the Microsoft philosophy, and the Linux philosophy in more human terms...
UNIX came first, saw a huge bundle of potential, and shared it with anyone who was interested. People got together, learned the ins and outs of UNIX, and contributed freely. There was no cost and no restriction, because these people understood that intentionally limiting knowledge is extremely stupid.
Apple came along. It saw the world of computing and thought it could do better, and so it went off all by its lonesome into a cave and started working away. Eventually, it came out with something functional that also looked very pretty.
"How did you do that?" Apple was asked. "I'm not telling you," replied Apple. "Can we help make it better?" "No." "Can we extend the OS ourselves?" "Only if you bring it to me first and have me approve it." "Can I show this to my friend?" "No, have him talk to me, instead."
Microsoft saw Apple's success and was also interested in making something amazing. Microsoft was also very smart and capable, and so he also went off by himself to work on his own. But he thought Apple was being an asshat by refusing to show people how to improve his OS, so when Microsoft unveiled his masterpiece, he also gave people the option of making it look the way they wanted it to look and to make their own software for it in any way they could make work for the OS.
Linux saw that Apple and Microsoft were very smart, but he didn't see why they had to be so condescending as to keep the development of their OS's to themselves, as though they were the only ones smart enough to know what makes an OS great. For this reason, Linux made an excellent product and then proceeded to show exactly how every aspect of his OS worked and asked people to help. It was an instant success, and people gave a huge collaborative effort to improve the OS and make nifty software packages for it.
Apple and Microsoft were still pretty brilliant and made original software and features that made their OS sell, but it was a shame that they refused to work with everyone else. Everyone working with Linux quickly emulated the features they liked, polished them up a bit, and shared their methods with the rest of the world for free.
Microsoft became very successful because he asked a bunch of very intelligent friends to give a solid effort toward developing some original ideas, especially entertainment. He made some mistakes every now and again, some larger than others, but he still does well for himself because he lets people do whatever they want to the OS other than changing it or seeing how it works.
Apple was an egotistical dick to start, and stayed an egotistical dick through today. He only ever worked by himself and refused to let just about everyone help him. Every now and again, he'd wander out of his cave with something cool, because he was still pretty smart, but he grew to be bitterly jealous of Microsoft's success.
Even though most of the world used Microsoft's and Apple's products, anyone who looked into the development of computing that UNIX was by far the reigning champion of the essentials of computing, and its splinter projects were the most trusted, most stable, and most secure when it really came down to it. Microsoft took BSD's network stack and other networking mechanics, and Apple switched entirely to a UNIX foundation. The most legendarily stable machines were overwhelmingly running some flavor of BSD as well. Most web servers, especially larger ones that needed to be extremely efficient and stable, either used a UNIX web server called Apache or some implementation thereof. Microsoft and Apple are gradually working in brilliant new features that UNIX geeks have already enjoyed for years, and the UNIX users all had a knowing chuckle when Microsoft and Apple got so excited about things UNIX had developed long ago.

Guns, not people, kill people? No, it really is the people. So many more crimes are committed with objects of opportunity such as belts, chairs, table legs, cars, and the baseball bat in your trunk, than with guns. And of the violent crimes committed with guns, the majority of them have been illegally obtained. Furthermore, you can easily spot areas in your city that are more prone to witnessing violent crime by looking for gun-free zones.
These facts should all tell you something: Some humans suck, and want to hurt you. Yes, someone who wants to kill you can use a gun to do so, but it's important to realize that if guns weren't around, he'd just as easily find some other way to do you in. It's human intent that kills, and it actually does it with weapons other than guns more often than with guns, especially legal ones. It's unfortunate that humans suck so much, but I'll give you an excellent reason why guns to more to solve this problem than aggravate it at the end of this post, as requested by Tapp.

Increasing gun control takes a self-defense option away from potential victims. I'm sorry, Ian, but bumping gun control laws doesn't make the multitude of weapons already in circulation magically disappear. There are even videos on YouTube of some crazy gangbangers chilling out at home with their eleven AK-47's. While there are still people with illegal firearms out there, I will insist upon legally owning my own firearm to defend myself. "The possibility of getting shot by an armed victim is a substantial deterrent to crime and prevents not only petty crime but physical confrontation as well from criminals."
"the number of Americans who report incidents where their guns averted a threat vastly outnumber those who report being the victim of a firearm-related crime."
"Opponents of lawful arming of individuals for self-defense argue that potential victims who present their weapons likely would escalate a confrontation and suffer greater injury than had they been unarmed. However, the opposite has been found to be true: armed potential victims have a significantly greater likelihood over unarmed or totally weapon-less victims of escaping criminal assault uninjured. The United States Department of Justice, Bureau of Justice Statistics, found that 'A fifth of the victims defending themselves with a firearm suffered an injury, compared to almost half of those who defended themselves with weapons other than a firearm or who had no weapon.'"
That last quote actually has an easily-found source: http://www.ojp.usdoj.gov/bjs/pub/ascii/hvfsdaft.txt

Quote: iangb, 2008-07-02T01:31:31Z

Sure, but the gun is the ideal self-defense tool because it's the Great Equalizer, as previously discussed. We could just as easily take away steak knives and say that you could just press really hard with a butterknife, couldn't we? After all, a steak knife only serves to cut through thick meat just as a gun only serves to fling lethal metal piece of metal at the speed of sound. Quote: iangb, 2008-07-02T01:31:31Z
Guns are specifically designed to kill people efficiently.
Excuuuuuuuse me... guns are specifically designed to kill living things. People use guns for hunting non-humans, y'know.
And chainsaws, as previously mentioned, are specifically designed to cause massive structural damage. Steak knives are specifically designed to slice through flesh.
I understand somewhat if you were to say that the gun has no purpose other than pointing at things for the purpose of destroying them, and you may call it odd that I enjoy target shooting as a sport, but I don't think it's any much more wierd than a baseball bat. Baseball bats are designed to hit things really hard, so if you confine guns to only shooting non-people, why aren't you so fervent about baseball bats being illegal to own and operate outside of a baseball stadium? Baseball bats are a very popular object of opportunity - husbands beat their wives with them all the time. Gangsters like beating on other gangs with aluminum bats. People even keep bats around for self defense.
Baseball bats and guns have this in common - both are intended for purely destructive purposes, and both happen to be very good weapons for self-defense. You ever been hit by a baseball bat? Those things could easily kill, and it's a slower, more painful death than a baseball bat. The difference, though, is that the bat is a melee weapon, which means that people of various sizes are variably competent with them. But no one can dodge bullets and people commonly wear kevlar, so to a gun, everyone's AC is 10. If some musclehead charged me with a bat and I had a bat of my own, I wouldn't like my chances. But I'd feel very comfortable if I had a gun, even if he did as well.

Quote: iangb, 2008-07-02T01:31:31Z

No. One in two people armed with non-guns suffered injuries in violent encounters, versus one in five who were armed. Assuming that you don't kill me instantly in the surprise round, you'll probably prefer not tack murder onto your record... but I'm standing and waiting for you to screw up because any homicide I commit in such a case would be justifiable. If you're attacking me and we both have guns, and you'd prefer not to have to kill me, then I actually don't like your chances.

In the LA riots of 1992, several city blocks were surprisingly untouched by rioters. Y'know why? Because their residents were lingering near their door ways, visibly holding shotguns. Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-07-02T01:51:17Z
Bombs really shouldn't be illegal until someone detonates them?
Keeping something legal until it's used to kill was never mentioned by me or any other pro-gunner as a sane idea. Bombs are terrible at conventional self-defense, and so they clearly wouldn't be used as such. They are very clearly a weapon of war with no street self-defense value, and not even of much value for an armed robbery or mugging. If somehow grenade-throwing ranges become popular, then I'd totally support it, but I'd definitely argue to keep them off the streets.

Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-07-02T01:51:17Z
Where do you draw the line when it comes to devices which can kill?
It's actually much simpler than you think: if the weapon is only useful to attack and/or destroy, and cannot be used as a conventional self-defense item, then make it illegal for everyday possession and use. A minigun cannot be carried with you and pulled out when you need to defend yourself, so it's clearly not a valid self-defense weapon and should be illegal to own. Since most violent encounters happen at conversation distance, explosives are entirely out of the realm of practicality for self-defense, especially because they damage things around the target just about always, so they, too, should be illegal. Railguns, M82's, flamethrowers, rocket launchers, bricks of C4, etc., are not practical self-defense items that only deal damage to an aggressor, so they should all be illegal.
I shudder for the same reason as Ian to use the term, but it really is common sense.

Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-07-02T01:51:17Z

This is "a device which can do nothing but commit a crime," so you're saying that "removing that device could prevent the crime altogether instead of waiting for a body to hit the floor and prosecuting the attacker" is the better idea, right?
Too few of you guys are anti-bat.
Mr. Whittacker's belt is only used to keep his pants up and to belt his wife when she forgets to cook dinner for him. Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-07-02T01:51:17Z

A common assumption is that people who are against gun control are also eager to hand them out to every Sam, Dick, and Harriet on the street. Just as I'm uncomfortable with gun-clueless people owning firearms, so am I uncomfortable with how many convicted violent criminals and repeat offenders are still allowed to purchase guns. I don't think domestic crimes should put you on a blacklist unless it happens repeatedly and the crime was particularly aggravated, but I do think that anyone convicted of a violent crime should be blacklisted, as should people who have demonstrated severe addiction to mind-altering substances. Ideally, I'd have every prospective gun owner go through a personal mental evaluation, but I know that we just don't have those kinds of resources at present, but that gives you an idea of what I think about who should be owning firearms.

Quote: brocerius, 2008-07-02T10:47:33Z
Firstly, as South Park said, "You cant run from a .44. Go ahead and try." Guns are ranged weapons, which are rare today.
You can, actually. If you take a trip down to the range, you'll find that it's actually much more difficult to hit something at, say, 5-7 yards than you'd think, and that's when you have all the time in the world to aim and you don't have adrenaline in your bloodstream.
One of my friends (a certified NRA instructor, actually) is a big fan of combat pistol courses, and he tells me all the time how much things change when you're under pressure. When he's shooting calmly, you can expect him to fire pretty near the bullseye even as far as 10 yards, but he said he may as well have been shooting with his eyes closed in aforementioned courses.
There's one in particular I remember him talking about that involved the instructor way off to the side of the range behind a curtain and all the participants starting with their guns at their sides and on safety. The instructor then burst out from behind the curtain and started sprinting down range with targets on the firing lanes keeping pace, and the students simply had to draw their weapons, load a mag, and fire. In the time it took the guy to run 30-40 yards, my friend and a few others were the only ones who even got any shots off before the target got to point-blank, and both of the shots he fired missed.

All the same, I'd still prefer to have a gun than to run from one. 50% of victims of violent crimes who weren't armed with a handgun were injured, versus 20% of the victims who were armed with a handgun, as I've already quoted and sourced.

I think it very much is, yes. The world becomes awesome because the world becomes that much more fair, and that much less barbaric. Gun control helps the strong keep a monopoly on force.

I think there's not enough distinction between "aggressor" and "defender".
The next time you're hearing someone tell you about a mugging, or watching a movie where someone's being mugged or something, take the time to analyze the scene. Which of the people is asking the other to give up his money? That dude's the aggressor. The guy forking it over? He's the defender. Or in another case, imagine yourself sitting in your own home and watching Friends on the couch after a long day at work when someone smashes in a window and starts climbing in. I want you to sort of pause that image in your mind. In that freeze-frame, which of you is forcibly entering a home that isn't theirs? Is it you, the guy watching television in the privacy of your home? Or is it the guy who just forcibly entered a home that isn't his?
I understand that there might be circumstances that could prevent a person from spotting these things immediately, as an otherwise normal human might, but there are medications and confidence-building exercises that can help motivate one to succeed in the world regardless.

Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-07-02T11:07:05Z
If something can be used offensively to shoot stuff, it can also be used defensively to shoot stuff.
Among the numerous examples I gave of impractical self-defense weapons, none of them followed the "a good offense makes a good defense" idea. Miniguns are damned heavy and usually have to be mounted, and it'd probably take you a while to load it and rev it up before the encounter ended. C4 is difficult to prime under stress, and it's difficult to escape the blast radius in a face-to-face encounter without your attacker suspecting you might be up to something. The barrel of an M82 would likely extend past the target were you to whip one out in self defense while you're being attacked (although on the flip side, it's difficult to mug someone at a distance of 300 yards... "okay, now throw your wallet really, really hard!")

Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-07-02T11:07:05Z

#451458 98 2008-07-07T19:09:09Z Strife
paperflyer,

For starters, welcome!
And also, thanks for the amazing input. It's refreshing, to say the least (and I was getting bored arguing with smartalco about his insistence on using his sippy-cup).

I fully agree with you that Windows has historically had enormous issues with backwards compatibility. I've needed all kinds of wacky emulators and OS hacks to get older software and games working in newer versions of Windows, and all because they insist on effectively starting fresh with each version of Windows. As I understand it, Linux and OS X have been very good about not making drastic changes to the kernel and offering ineffective compatibility modes to run older software.
But I think that's something that Windows should do better, and not something that Windows should do like OS X. This is primarily because I don't like Apple's interface guidelines ("requirements" would be more accurate), because it drastically limits creativity. If I make a damned good GUI for my program but it doesn't meet some technicality in the "Human Interface Guidelines," I'm not going to bother releasing it for OS X. The judge of what makes a good GUI is not objective rules imposed by the guys who wrote the OS, but by the users themselves - if I didn't make a good GUI, then I won't have a large userbase and my product will suffer. It's simple economics.

#451638 152 2008-07-08T19:13:54Z Strife
Quote: iangb, July 08, 2008 07:13 am
...it's a deist point of view. Or pantheistic view, which states that the universe is part of God (for if this was not the case, there would be an entity greater than god: that of God and the universe combined)

'God' is not always a personal being. Only in the traditional sense of the word.

That's exactly my point - 'pantheism' is as much theism as agnosticism is a form of atheism. It's in popular usage, but it's an inconsistent concept.
If I say, "I believe that God is the trees," it's unfortunate how many people in this world will dignify that. If I equate "god" with the abstract noun that is the set of all trees, then I'm being misleading. I can't credit my "god" with creation, sentience, or anything else that is the bare minimum for an idea to be considered a religion, which makes it a non-theistic view. Similarly, the only real philosophy you can draw from pantheism is que ser, ser, which is just like having no religion to begin with. The "god" of pantheism is simply a concept, an abstract container that humans have invented to categorize things. It's not something that could possibly influence the world except by using it to categorize other things and organize your thoughts. Calling [everything] "god" is as misleading as calling the concept of removing staples "god," or the act of tripping over a thing you have yourself placed on the ground within the last seven seconds "god."

#451767 159 2008-07-09T05:07:17Z Strife
Quote: iangb, July 08, 2008 07:15 pm
No, I meant the Gaia as popularised by Final Fantasy: The Spirits Within - essentially, the belief is deifying the organism as put forwards by Scythe's link. The world is an organism - God.

To be honest, it was the first blurred-line religion that aproimates both pantheism and a personal god that I could think of. I think the better half of my post was asking for how you define 'theology', because it could well be that we have different opinions about it's definition. I'm mainly going on pantheism as theism because of the nomenclature.

To get this out of the way first, I will of course agree that by nomenclature, pantheism would be considered a theology, but it's as good a theology as cheese-worship, making it effectively a non-theology. When I call it a non-theistic idea, I'm merely saying that it's absurd, but it obviously describes something that people think is a theology.

My definition of theology is the underlying belief or system of beliefs of something that could potentially be a religion, and the best definition for a religion I've heard is something like "a system of ideas pertaining to matters of ultimate concern." I'd say the bare minimum requirement for a religion is belief in at least one sentient entity that has at least one supernatural quality, and the relationship of that entity or those entities to humans produces some general suggested ways to live your life (for example, God is your creator and means well for you, so do what he says; Yezidi devil worshippers acknowledge that devils have power they don't, so they bribe them). Religions also very frequently have some concept of life after death other than "your body rots in the ground; game over," scripture, and parables, but I'm not so convinced that they're required.

Why, what's yours?

#451875 5 2008-07-09T20:57:44Z Strife
This happens to me every time I try to use IRC in Windows, regardless of client (and believe me, I've tried many just to see if that's what was wrong), so just downloading mIRC isn't going to solve anything.
...or at least it shouldn't, if the problem is what I think it is.
But unfortunately, I'll have to conclude that I don't *actually* know what exactly the problem is, just that it's the same one I have. I only use Windows for gaming, so I just haven't taken the time to debug anything yet, so I haven't found a fix or even looked into what's happening.

It doesn't sound like it's a permissions issue with the application itself, because it can clearly go online. But it might still be a blocked port issue if waypasteleven is trying to bounce something back to you, is failing, and therefore drops your connection. Go to Start > Control Panel > Security > Windows Firewall > Ports (or whatever those exact names are; I'm not using a Windows machine right now), and add a port for the IRC connection (6667, is it?), and let us know if that solves anything.

Other questions:
Are you trying to do this at home? (My lab blocks all ports commonly used for chat, such as those for AIM and IRC.)
Do you use a router to connect to the internet, or is the cable coming straight from your wall and into your computer without anything else between them?
Go to Start > Run, type in "cmd" in the box (without quotes), and click Run. In the window that appears (it should say something like "C:\Windows\system32\>"), type in (without quotes) "ping waypasteleven.com" and hit enter, then paste what it spits back at you here.

#453367 20 2008-07-17T08:57:59Z Strife
Quote: ZeroTruths, July 15, 2008 06:34 pm
Well, now I have a dilemma.
I've found two laptops that are good, and cost the same. Problem is, I'm undecided as to which one I should get.
Gateway
HP Special Edition

Comments?

This is a non-issue; even though I normally side with NVIDIA, the ATI card kicks the ever-loving Christ out of the NVIDIA card.
http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparison_of..._3xxx.29_series
http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparison_of...Force_8M_series

Notice also that the ATI's fillrates are far in excess of the NVIDIA's maximae, and the maximum bandwidth is measured on a higher order of magnitude entirely.

I would highly, highly suggest you go with the Gateway if you're considering one of those two.
