#20855 1 2008-07-21T21:59:19Z Strife
To warn you, this is mostly just venting.
I'm a federali code-monkey, and the latest bit of annoyance I've had to deal with is creating an Acquisition Report template in LaTeX (the typesetting language, not the stable dispersion of polymer microparticles). My boss has recently been shown that LaTeX exists, and now he's going crazy and demanding that everything use it, and he's volunteered me to help. Thing is, I don't know jack about LaTeX. Or more accurately, I don't have any experience with LaTeX. So I've devoted pretty much the entirety of this work day so far to learning it.

SPOILER: It fucking blows.

First off, I am amazed to discover this new culture in which people get so excited about fonts and typesetting. If you skim through some usenet posts on the subject, you'll find people who dedicate their lives to arguing just how many pica of spacing is necessary after a wide variety of punctuation marks depending upon the context. And then it hit me that there is probably an entire department of losers working for Microsoft who have zero other reason to be on the payroll other than their work in maintaining and refining truetype fonts. To me, this seems about as useful as employing people to count the blades of grass on the front lawn, ensuring via a set of rules made as convoluted as possible that there is a prime number of blades at any given moment, and that their summed lengths exactly equal the number of tire treads in the senior staff parking lot.
If you are this fascinated by fonts, I highly advise that you kill yourself.

Secondly, I had no idea that people gave so much of a collective shit about spaces. Before LaTeX, I only knew of 'a space', like the kind I put after words and sentences and shit. But now I've been introduced to this wide collection of "word spaces" and "sentence spaces" and "thickspaces" and "thinspaces," and I realize that in the eyes of these people who care about these little tiny fucking nuances, my typesetting has been in flagrant violation of all that is holy in the typesetting bible. Did you know there are at least three kinds of dashes, too? See, I've only been using the one --- the single hyphen --- but apparently that's incorrigible. The rule is: a single hyphen for compound words such as in "a double-hyphen for number ranges," a double-hyphen for number ranges as in "Snape kills Dumbledore in pp. 348--349," and a triple-hyphen for references, as used above around "the single hyphen." And y'know that little thingy in the corner of your keyboard, just below the F1 key and on the same key as the squiggly key? That little guy that you press when you want to get to the console in many developer-friendly games? That little bastard, who is so far the fuck in the keyboard's Alaskan wilderness that it destroys the flow of your touch-typing every time you have to press him, is the preferred left quote character. This means that every time I've typed ["rapably soft"], I should've typed [``rapably soft"]. Yep yep - two characters as the left quote, one on the right. Seems unbalanced to me, but I didn't make the rule.

Thirdly, since typesetting is all there is to this bitch, that's all the manual talks about. But it has to be thorough and it has to make itself look comprehensive, so it draws out every single possible fucking variation in typesetting as much as it can. Reading this thing is like listening to your grandpa (the one with senile dementia) talk about every last one of his fishing stories, all that he's ever lived through, heard, or made up, in a single, unstoppable, slowly-advancing wall of pain. This thing is nothing short of pure, unrelenting agony stitched into the fibers of the parchment. No one but the most diehard fanatic of typesetting can read this without having to take a break every paragraph, and you'd have to be just fascinated, just ass-to-the-windshield fascinated with typesetting to read the entire thing in one go, which is what I'm being asked to do, and I quite simply lack that amount of enthusiasm.

And finally, the language itself has to be one of the most contrived and ungeneralized I've ever personally come into contact with. Every little change you want to make has its own name, its own fucking 'environment' to work in. For example --- in every language where the backslash is an escape character, how do you write an actual backslash? In every sane language, it's just two backslashes: '\\'. But not in LaTeX, it isn't. No siree-bob. You wanna know what it is in LaTeX? Do you really, really wanna know? It's '\textasciibackslash'. "Text ASCII motherfucking Backslash." But oh, that's just the beginning. For every little bit of whitespace you could possibly cram in before, after, and in between punctuation, or even indentation, there's a unique command.
\spaceafterleftmarginbutbeforebulletpoint{5pt}
\spacebetweenbulletpointandtextbutnotbetweenleftmarginandbulletpoint{4pt}
\spacebothbetweenbulletpointandtextaswellasleftmarginandbulletpoint[prettypleasewithsugarontop]{8pt}
These same shenanigans are exactly why I ended up turning my back on Perl. "I want Perl to pick up my daughter after school." "No problem! Just set the environmental variable called dollarsign-chinese-horse-symbol to reference a filehandle that points to a file named three backspace characters and you're good to go!" Every function, every single way of fixing a problem, has its own unique name and implementation, and that's just plain unacceptable for something that allegedly deserves to be called usable.

In complete honesty, I don't have any qualms about using MS Word, or open-source equivalent, for my typesetting needs, primarily because I don't exhibit such an excessive amount of caring, and I really don't think it'll be to my detriment unless the guy at my job interview is on the LaTeX dev team and has a stroke after seeing my resume, but then I probably wouldn't want to work with him anyway.

And now, back to work.
*sigh*
Shit sucks.

Quote: squibbles, 2008-01-24T13:09:42Z
Actually, he's right now that i thin about it, unless your game is BlackJack, Slots, or Snakes and ladders, which all rely on simple random number statements, don't use basic...i remember making a game of Snake once..that sucked, by the time i was finished, i wanted to kill microsoft for Visual Basic.
What's with all this BASIC hating? It's easy to learn. Yes, actually trying to do things with it is a little more troublesome, but awesome games can still be made.
http://www.blitzbasic.com/
Check "Community Posts" and actually play some of the games.
I don't think it teaches bad habits. Blitz Basic is like a BASIC/C hybrid. The language is dumbed down, but advanced processes and ideas like types and functions are well organized and easier to use than in C. Also, you don't need to learn sentential values or signifiers, like \n for newline. The Blitz3D downloadable demo also contains a complete command reference in case you do get lost. The only reason I haven't made any games on it is because I'm lazy and I use MGB (MyGameBuilder) instead.
http://mygamebuilder.com/
Point is, if you take some time and learn the lay of the land in BASIC, you can do stuff with it.

Wow, that was my longest post in like...forever.

I can assure you that it is not uncommon for experienced and professional software developers to advise newbies to steer clear of BASIC. Y'think that might be for a reason, or did they all just spin a wheel of languages to help decide on a random language to collectively hate?

Mike has a treehouse. Your incentive for wanting to be in Mike's treehouse is to hang out with Mike. Everyone you know says Mike is a pretty cool guy (eh owns a treehouse and doesn't afraid of anything).
You ask Mike if you can come into his treehouse, and he responds that you can as long as you do something for him. You ask what that thing is. #455909 581 2008-07-29T21:37:26Z Strife
Quote: incluye, 2008-07-29T19:26:10Z

There are ways of making people more efficient, yet evolution has not yet done so because there isn't a lot of evolutionary pressure, and people whose appendices burst nowadays don't usually suffer any in the genepool.
Well, that's operating on the presupposition "Evolution is what really happened."

Quote: incluye, 2008-07-29T19:26:10Z
However, if IC is correct, we can't tell - because we'll always be saying, "It has to have evolved."
You're assuming that evolutionists will resort to the low, unscientific way of doing things used by proponents of creationism.
This is the primary difference between how science works and how creationism works (or Intelligent Design, if creationism decides to put on a cheap rental suit):
Science will make a hypothesis and then do everything in its power to prove it false. If after years of study it is unable to find the hypothesis false, it reluctantly accepts the hypothesis as a theory, but it will continue to be tested long afterwards, especially as science finds more ways to try to disprove it. If science is continually unsuccessful in falsifying the hypothesis, the hypothesis gets more credit. Evolution is scientific because there is a way to falsify it - if you find an account of an organism simply beginning to exist, or someone proves irreducible complexity beyond a shadow of a doubt, then evolution will be discredited, and science will move on. It is very important for a potential theory to be falsifiable, because that's the only way it can be proven with such a high degree of certainty.
Religion will start with an end goal in mind and then search for supporting evidence, often ignoring or attempting to explain away contradicting evidence with more made-up reasoning (and if that's questioned, things can get recursive). The declared truth will also usually be something that's unfalsifiable (whereas falsifiability is a requirement for scientific theories); the only way to disprove that there was a cosmic creator, for example, is to interrogate the creator. For this reason, it's rejected right from the start as a possible theory by science.
And lastly, science will always admit that a given theory could be wrong and can happily give you a list of things to look into if you want to prove it wrong, but religion will never back down from the truth it's trying to declare and refuses to believe that it could possibly be false.
You'll notice that an accredited scientist will never say, "it has to have evolved" unless he can provide evidence for it.
But you will notice that there's a history of self-proclaimed experts labeling just about anything irreducibly complex until some actual scientist can reduce its complexity.

To address your reply, though...
Irreducible complexity can never be scientifically correct because it's not scientific. It isn't falsifiable (so it can't be proven correct beyond a reasonable doubt). Hell, it isn't even testable. Coming to a scientific discussion with irreducible complexity is like coming to an IEEE conference with a block of wood; it isn't science, and you can expect to be ridiculed if not simply ignored outright.

I'm consistently amazed at how few people will notice this pattern between religion and science.
Religion: "Rain comes from God!"
Science: "No, it doesn't. It comes from [blah blah]."
Religion: "Oh. Well... fine, but you can't possibly explain where thunder comes from, therefore it must come from God!"
Science: "No, it doesn't. It comes from [blah blah]."
Religion: "Oh. Well... fine, but you can't possibly explain where moonlight and starlight come from, therefore it must come from God!"
Science: "No, it doesn't. It comes from [blah blah]."
Religion: "Oh. Well... fine, but you can't possibly explain where earthquakes come from, therefore they must come from God!"
Science: "No, they don't. They come from [blah blah]."
Religion: "Oh. Well... fine, but you can't possibly explain where feathers come from, therefore they must come from God!"
Science: "No, they don't. They come from [blah blah]."
Religion: "Oh. Well... fine, but you can't possibly explain where fish eyes come from, therefore they must come from God!"
Science: "No, they don't. They come from [blah blah]."
...ad infinitum.

Quote: incluye, 2008-07-29T19:26:10Z
But still, appendices, if evolution is true, have to have evolved for some reason, but they do nothing and burst if infected. I can imagine evolution giving us an extra finger or so, but such an organ as an appendix looks like a pretty glaring mistake.

Angel: "Okay, God, we've taken a look at this proposed design for a human as you requested..."
God: "Mmhmm?"
Angel: "Well, we just have a few questions. We're not sure we understand the genius of your design in some aspects of the human."
God: "Oh, don't worry; that's because it was my choice to make you so ignorant. What are your questions?"
Angel: "Well... as we understand it, the spine is pretty central to the whole body."
God: "That's correct."
Angel: "So why did you give it such a sharp curve from its base? That doesn't look like it's very good for support. Are they supposed to walk on all fours like the monkeys?"
God: "Good question. No, they're meant to walk on their two legs, but years from now, the humans will make a contraption called the x-ray scanner, and it will be able to give them a clear look at the shape of the spine. When they do that, they will see that beautiful curvatuve and know my grace."
Angel: "But won't it make them suffer for the thousands of years before they have the x-ray?"
God: "Oh. Probably, yeah. Anything else?"
Angel: "The coccyx. Why is it there?"
God: "Oh, haha. Yeah, that'll make it really painful to land on their butts. There's no humor in it if they're okay. It's not like you'd laugh at someone who just fell on his hands, would you?"
Angel: "We don't find suffering humorous, though."
God: "Well maybe you don't. Next question?"
Angel: "But wait, couldn't the humans get seriously injured if a piece of the coccyx broke off and entered the bloodstream?"
God: "I suppose."
Angel: "And wouldn't it be more likely to break, given that it's just sort of jutting out of the back without much padding?"
God: "Sure, but then it's also much less likely to hurt when they fall on their butts. It's sort of a trade-off."
Angel: "Okay then. How about the optic nerve?"
God: "Well that enables them to see, and there are two of them so they have depth perception..."
Angel: "No no, we get all that. We just wanted to know why the occipital lobe is all the way in the back even though the eyes are in the front."
God: "Yeaaaaah, the brain was by far the hardest part, and I had already finished designing it that way before I made the eyes, so I just sort of stretched that nerve back there. I may get around to fixing that later."
Angel: "Oh, okay. Um... let's see... oh, how about the appendix?"
God: "What about it?"
Angel: "Well... why is it there?"
God: "Ah, another excellent question. You see, I'm going to make a whole bunch of tiny creatures called bacteria. They'll be so small that there can be billions of them inside a human. And depending upon what kinds of how many there are, the humans can get really sick."
Angel: "Oh, so it helps keep the bad bacteria out! Brilliant!"
God: "Well no, actually. I mean, sure, it does help somewhat, but not really enough to make much of a difference."
Angel: "Oh."
God: "Yeah, see, the appendix is in a really good spot to collect some really nasty bacteria, and it's a perfect breeding site for them because the rest of the body is essentially going to ignore it. And it's especially excellent because it doesn't do anything else, so the human won't even know it's happening. And then when it gets full enough... BAM! It explodes, and it sends torrents upon torrents of infectious bacteria through the system, with massive internal bleeding, to boot!"
Angel: "But then why would you include it?"
God: "Are you questioning the genius of my design?"
Angel: "I- no, it just doesn't seem like it's ne-"
God: "Did I create you to be so arrogant?"
Angel: "Well y- I mean... no? I mean yes..."
God: "What is your name!?"
Angel: "It's Lucifer, Your Worship."
God: "GTFO MY HEAVEN."
Angel: "B-but... there's nowhere else to go!"
God: "Oh, don't you worry. I'll make a place, alright."
...and that, children, is the real story behind Hell and Satan.

#20949 1 2008-07-30T18:35:28Z Strife
Washington Post Article

I actually heard this on the radio on my drive to work (I like to listen to the Christian Satellite Networks broadcast in my area); the Washington Post article was one of the first Google results.

The issue here is, in brief:
The American Civil Liberties Union is threatening to file a federal lawsuit against the US Navy unless they put a stop to their 160-year tradition of holding a "voluntary" mealtime prayer in the Naval Academy.

This all started because a handful of shipmen complained to the ACLU that they felt pressured to join the prayer session.

My take on this:
All of the Christian organizations that oppose the ACLU here keep calling the event voluntary, and I think that's very misleading; that's in fact the issue here. Just because you are free to say a prayer from any religion you want, to recite the humanist manifesto, or to sit idly for the 60 seconds, does not make it voluntary. It isn't voluntary, and it is misleading to call it voluntary, because the food is still withheld for clearly religious reasons for 60 seconds, and those who don't want to partake in the purely religious event should be able to get their food right away. Even if they were to call it a pre-mealtime moment of reflection, it's still pretty clearly a time for religious activity.
What I don't think should happen: Religious actions should be strictly prohibited among service members.
What I do think should happen: Everyone sits down and gets their food, without a dedicated 60 seconds. At that point, those who don't want to do anything religious don't have to and can begin eating, and those who want to pray, recite the humanist manifesto, or have a moment of personal reflection may do so as part of their personal time when they might otherwise choose to eat.

Ultimately, it's a technicality; I don't think I'd actually care were I in the Navy Academy, but I do agree with the ACLU on this on principle.

Other thoughts?

#459053 17 2008-08-07T19:01:32Z Strife
Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-08-07T13:10:41Z
Agnosticism isn't about belief or disbelief. In fact, it's denying the choice to believe or disbelieve. To affirm atheism, you have to go out on faith that the evidence you have for the nonexistence of God is perfectly complete. You have to say "Alright, I may not have the answers, but I think the amount of evidence suggests that God doesn't exist." You'll find a similar regard with theists. They say "I may not have all the answers, but I think the amount of evidence suggests that God does exist."

The final step in affirmation for theism or atheism is a step of faith, to fill in the evidence which does not exist to derive an answer. But this doesn't mean that atheism is a weaker argument!

Epistemology, that is, our acquisition of knowledge, is always a final leap of faith. No information is absolutely complete. This is not to say it's foolish to learn things because there's never complete knowledge. In fact, it's usually a pretty good idea to take what you know and apply it, at some point. But that requires a final cut in logic, where you disavow the possibilty of evidence to the contrary in the remaining gap of things not known about the subject. For something like "the sky makes rain", the gap you have to cross is infinitely narrow. For something like "evolution", it's still pretty narrow, but a shade wider. For something like "God", the gap in our complete knowledge is quite wide.

Agnosticism is choosing not to cross that logical gap in either direction, and it can pertain to knowledge beyond that of God. It can really pertain to any subject.

Sure, at some point, it's wise to cross that gap. But agnostics feel that it's not wise to cross the logical gap of missing knowledge about God. For some, agnosticism is as good as atheism. If you have yet to cross the logical gap to say something exists, and that something will have zero effect on your life until it exists, then indecision will evoke the same behavior as choosing disbelief.
We might have some issues...

I'm sure you've heard my whole shtick about 'agnostic' and 'atheist' describing two completely different things, so I'm not going to repeat it and assume that you'll bring up any disagreements if you have 'em.
Point is, atheism doesn't need affirmation. If you were talking about antitheism, then I agree. Only claims that assert anything need affirmation, and atheism doesn't do that; there are no claims or beliefs to affirm. Even if you're talking about affirming a lack of belief, that's easy enough to do by simply pointing out that various theists' claims are unconvincing, and that right there completely justifies withholding belief.
So I think either you misspoke or there's a dead horse ain't been beaten enough.

I think a more accurate way of putting it would be that agnostics don't think there is (or possibly just don't see) a bridge over either gap. Whether the agnostic then decides to make a leap over one of the gaps is still up to him; plenty of Christian sects that are heavy on faith are agnostic in this regard.

#459103 23 2008-08-07T21:23:46Z Strife
Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-08-07T20:25:56Z
As for the Suki bit, we've all read that thing a hundred times over. And I say now what I said way back when. Language isn't prescriptive. I think that's probably what maddens you about the religion argument. There's some prescription you've devised for concepts and terms which is very close to a workable model, but models simply can't exist in the study of religion.
As a computational linguist, I'm going to have to disagree. I firmly believe that any "artistic" idea or otherwise one that doesn't have any explicit rules, you can always boil down to a complex set of explicit rules. I'm not suggesting that it'll be simple to do, that many will develop explicit rules before you localize to the neuron level, or that any of these aren't np-complete problems, but it obviously has to happen because human "intuition" is quantified. The struggle is in finding the most practical denominator to bring the system down to to make it workable for whatever your purpose is.

Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-08-07T20:25:56Z
There's some prescription you've devised
...
So, even though you want the terms "agnosticism" and "atheism" to refer to those things you made up; in the vernacular, they don't. And in the end, people who call themselves "agnostics" wouldn't agree with the definition you've attributed to them. So, do you assume your definition is wrong? No. You assume that those people need to change to use your definition of their beliefs. It's a wacky cycle, and yeah, I've watched you churn through it a number of times. It's the fundamental flaw of prescribing language. It's neat how you've crafted a binary system to house all possible belief systems using words which already exist, however.... those words aren't used that way. If you could come up with your own words for that neat binary concoction of yours and describe those words effectively, then you'd be on to something.

It uses some kind of crazy military tech to cancel out background noise entirely - a sensor touching your cheek picks up the vibrations in your mouth so it knows what sound is coming from you, as opposed to the background noise it picks up with a second mic pointed outward (a friend of mine with the same piece demonstrated this by calling while he was flying down the highway with windows open and music blaring, and I swear I thought he was calling from home). But the problem is that it's a little uncomfortable and the piece that's supposed to be in contact with my cheek isn't pushed into it. So I'm looking for alternatives.

Let's also limit this to < $200, if we could.

#464227 6 2008-08-25T01:00:57Z Strife
Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-08-24T23:33:48Z
I've said countless times that arguments persist only long enough to determine that they are semantic arguments, and then they stalemate.
Necessarily so?
How 'bout an argument about... whether eating tar is good for you. Once the parties argue for a few pages about what they mean by "tar" before come to a consensus (a chemical definition) that they're both talking about tar intended to pave roads, I hardly think that argument would end in a stalemate.

Otherwise, the subject of this thread has only been part of my experience; once the terms are agreed upon (I've even said things like, "when I say X, I mean [blah blah]," such as in the Definition of Atheism thread when I talk about "gnosticism"), then I argue for my case because I think I'm right and the view I'm arguing against is wrong. I try to avoid arguing about things that I don't think have a right answer.

I agree with Dave, but only partially - I think there are some arguments that will inevitably stalemate because there are no objectively right answers, but I think there are plenty more with objectively right answers.

Quote: blue_tetris, 2008-08-24T23:33:48Z
I feel bad for computational linguists. Plenty of things are objective, but language is necessarily subjective. We create it, expand it, and innovate on its terms every day. If a majority doesn't agree with a definition for a word, then in what way does that definition describe the word's usage?
I'll happily concede that inventive and artistic use of language can be subjective, but such a sweeping statement like "language is necessarily subjective" is falsified by the existence of languages such as Lojban, in which it's quite possible to speak entirely without ambiguity or even much need for context, which is otherwise impossible in naturally-developed languages.

One of the things that makes my job a whole lot easier, though, is a little thing called WordNet, which is essentially a massive flowchart of relations between words. Obviously, handling made-up words, verbed words, etc., is completely outside the scope of what I can cover, but I'm certain that day will come.
...because, as I've said previously, when you take anything subjective and boil it down enough, you get objectivity. If you don't think, as I don't, that anything but the physical brain is responsible for all things lingual in humans, then clearly the fact that a naturally-formed, deterministic machine (your brain) is the originator, maintainer, etc., of language, then even language is fundamentally objective.

#465123 12 2008-08-28T17:45:02Z Strife
These are the guidelines to Messageboard Games that I hope will be pinned by a mod.
  • Try not to post in more than 4 or 5 games at once. This is because then the "Last Posts" will be plastered with your name all over it, and you may shove popular games of the front page.
  • Don't create stupid games or games that have already been made. It's annoying, and they will be closed anyway.
  • Don't be stupid and corrupt good games with rape, ponies, or anything else that's inapporiate or idiotic. Some of my favorite games were closed because of this. And it's just stupid anyway.
  • Don't create games that faciliate spamming, and don't post extremely long or muliple posts. For obvious reasons.
  • Don't revive old threads. They're buried for a reason.
  • Try to stay on the low with the cursing. If you want to curse, go into B&T.
  • And finally, don't flame or personally attack a member. Light jokes are fine, but doing this is wrong in all of the forums, and will result in a warn, suspension, or in extreme cases, a ban.


I know that people might say that I am a hypocrite or an idiot for creating this, considering I used to spam regularly and even got warned, but hey, I've changed, and I want to stop anyone else from doing it said the

#466075 9 2008-08-31T21:15:37Z Strife
I highly, highly, highly recommend you download the driver installers somewhere (friend with internet / public library nearby?) and then transfer them to your computer and run them. There are many more things that go into installing hardware than moving .dll's around, and unless you know every last change that one of those installer packages will make (i.e. you wrote the drivers yourself), your hardware will probably not function as expected.

#467178 5 2008-09-04T23:57:51Z Strife
You can't write with a butterfly.
Besides which, butterflies totally die after a few days or something anyway, which is far below the life expectancy of a pen. Pens just keep on going, and you can replace the ink if you run out or even if it explodes. I'd like to see a butterfly get a fresh swap of its internal organs after it lies in the gutter for a year covered in mud and work perfectly fine.
And pens may explode in your pocket on a warm day, sure, but consider that a butterfly would be ripped apart in your pocket as soon as you put it in there, and Lord knows butterfly internals can stain just as much as pen ink (oh, and ink is better at that, too).
Pens are extremely useful in day-to-day life, and butterflies are only there to flap around and look pretty sometimes (the rest of the time, they're a nuisance). Either way, a pen can be used to draw things even prettier than butterflies anyway, so that's not even a point for the butterflies.

The only thing pens don't have going for them is their inability to fly. But that's exactly how I prefer it - it'd be annoying as hell having to catch a flying pen whenever you needed to write something down.

Forget butterflies. Pens are superior.

The closest I've come to naming my hard drives is giving them mounting points on my hard drive...
/ - the HD with the OS on it.
/pata - the PATA hard drive I used to use (300 GB), now devoted to media storage
/stor - general storage

I do, however, name a lot of my electronic devices.

JEBUS was my very first laptop. He was a Toshiba Satellite Pro S35, or something like that. He died slowly. There's a thread or two about him around these parts somewhere.

ghetto-jesus was made of salvaged parts, and cost me ~$0. He was an outdated and hideous desktop, and I had to do all kinds of unstable hacks to get him running at a desirable speed. He's currently laying in a dump somewhere in Boston, stripped of his case and a few hardware bits.

jehobah is my current desktop. I purchased each piece on newegg and put him together about 10 months ago. He's my general-use and extreme gaming machine (Crysis at 60 fps, etc.). He has Linux on him, but I haven't used it in ages because I only use him for web-browsing and gaming, and cygwin fills in the rare requirement for something UNIX would do better.

strife is my ThinkPad T61. He has Linux and Vista installed, although I haven't used Vista in bloody ages. He's a general-use laptop, but particularly set up for software development & miscellaneous coding tasks. He can play a lot of games just fine, as well, but obviously nothing intensive.

x61s-nps is the ThinkPad X61s I use for work. It's owned by the Naval Postgraduate School and it's an X61s, hence the name. It runs only Ubuntu Linux, and I don't really use it a whole lot outside of work.

azazel is my Tungsten T/X palm device. I thought I'd use him more than I do, but these days I use it to play Space Trader while I shit. I mean, every now and again I've looked up a friend's street address or checked my schedule, but mostly it sits around until I need to have another bowel movement. He does, however, see extensive use during roadtrips or when I have some serious work to do that requires me to stay mobile.

dispater is a network drive that mysteriously vanished. He used to share multimedia with the rest of my family. I think the maid "organized" it away into some obscure hiding spot, as she does.

Chester A. Arthur is the desk chair in the room that used to be mine, and for a desk that I used to use (which now holds the family computer). His name was Susan at first, but in building him I noticed that he had a certain male-specific part.

I should name my PSP...

Everyone sits in a circle with five fingers up (or more if the group is bigger). The turn goes around the circle, and each person says something they've never done. #469045 186 2008-09-11T17:41:02Z Strife
Quote: incluye, 2008-09-11T15:41:06Z
Quote: the_psychohazard, September 10 @  2008 11:25pm
When people accept the fact that human life is largely meaningless and insignificant, and can be comfortable living their life knowing this, that is truly finding peace and meaning in your own life.
So, apparently, the meaning of life is to realize that you're meaningless? Hahaha.
It could be the meaning, except that as that statement says, there is no meaning. In other words, such a situation would never happen if it's true that life is meaningless (unless you mean, "the meaning of life is to incorrectly believe that life is meaningless").

But seriously, kiddies, this is like asking, "what is the meaning of a chair?" There is no meaning to a chair - it's just a frickin' chair, and there isn't much more to it than that. (The chair's purpose is to serve as a thing to sit upon comfortably. Incidentally, it can also be used to stand on, to block doors, and to beat one's spouse, but those are not its purpose.)

If you do not believe that life was created, and you believe that life just happened (as I do), then there is no reason for you to believe that life has a purpose.
If you believe that life was created, then I suppose you'd believe whatever the hell your religion would have you believe (e.g. Christians should believe that the purpose of human life is to worship God).
But I don't want to hear any more of this self-referential nonsense like "the purpose of life is to be compassionate toward all other life" or "the purpose of Strife's life is to hate everyone."

But before one of you chuckleheads decides to say that the purpose of life is to know God, I'll warn you now that I'll throw a goddamned chair at you if you say it. If the purpose of life is to know God, then this means that God has a problem - He does not know what He is - and so he designed life as a sort of convoluted computer that will help him crank out the answer to His question of what He is. If you still want to say it's to know God, give an argument; otherwise, expect a chair to the cranium.

And, again, I hate all of you.

#21497 1 2008-09-16T19:58:49Z Strife
I'm bored enough to care about this.

"I would never abuse any of my powers, as, my mind would stop me."
This is not a valid grammatical construct.

When "as" is used to mean "because" or "since," there is no comma after it. Would you put a comma after "because" in that sentence? After "since?"
I've heard you say this phrase aloud, and it sounds just as awkward spoken. I know you make a pause after "as," but pauses like that are neither accurate indications that a comma should be used nor sensible in the first place. It sort of seems to me that you use "as" so frequently because you think it sounds more sophisticated or intelligent, but when you overuse it, and overuse it incorrectly, it has exactly the opposite effect.
This use of "as" is not used anywhere in any literature I've seen. You made it up, and continue using it even though it has never been validated by any other literate English-speaker.

In fact, in most situations wherein you use "as," even if you would have excluded the comma that follows, using "as" is more awkward than "since" or "because." Even a frickin' semicolon would work much better.
Assuming that the above sentence even makes sense, the following are far more acceptable:
"I would never abuse any of my powers because my mind would stop me."
"I would never abuse any of my powers since my mind would stop me."
"I would never abuse any of my powers; my mind would stop me."
But the one you actually went with doesn't make a lick of grammatical sense.

Even your um-uh-like-um's are more tolerable than this.

Anyway, I wanted to address this habit of yours because I think you may have gone a little too long thinking it was valid English.
